Sharp values for the constants in the polynomial Bohnenblust-Hille
inequalityThanks: G.A. Muñoz-Fernández and J.B. Seoane-Sepúlveda were

Abstract.

   In this paper we prove that the complex polynomial Bohnenblust-Hille
   constant for
   [MATH: <semantics><mn>2</mn><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">2</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   -homogeneous polynomials in
   [MATH: <semantics><msup><mi>ℂ</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\mathbb{C}}^{2}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   is exactly
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mroot><mfrac><mn>3</mn><mn>2</mn></mfrac><mn>4</mn></mroot>
   <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\sqrt[4]{\frac{3}{2}}</annotation></semant
   ics> :MATH]
   . We also give the exact value of the real polynomial Bohnenblust-Hille
   constant for
   [MATH: <semantics><mn>2</mn><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">2</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   -homogeneous polynomials in
   [MATH: <semantics><msup><mi>ℝ</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\mathbb{R}}^{2}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   . Finally, we provide lower estimates for the real polynomial
   Bohnenblust-Hille constant for polynomials in
   [MATH: <semantics><msup><mi>ℝ</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\mathbb{R}}^{2}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   of higher degrees.

Key words and phrases:

   Bohnenblust–Hille constants, Absolutely summing operators, Quantum
   Information Theory

2010 Mathematics Subject Classification

   46G25, 47L22, 47H60.

1. Preliminaries: What you need to know

   Any homogeneous polynomial in
   [MATH: <semantics><msup><mi>𝕂</mi><mi>n</mi></msup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\mathbb{K}}^{n}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   (
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mi>𝕂</mi><mo>=</mo><mi>ℝ</mi></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\mathbb{K}}={\mathbb{R}}</annotation></se
   mantics> :MATH]
   or
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>ℂ</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\mathbb{C}}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   ) of degree
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mi>m</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi>ℕ</mi></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">m\in{\mathbb{N}}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   can be written as
   (1.1)
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo
   rspace="0.111em">=</mo><mrow><munder><mo
   movablelimits="false">∑</mo><mrow><mrow><mo>|</mo><mi>α</mi><mo>|</mo><
   /mrow><mo>=</mo><mi>m</mi></mrow></munder><mrow><msub><mi>a</mi><mi>α</
   mi></msub><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mi>x</mi><mi>α</mi></msup></mrow></mrow></mro
   w><mo>,</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">P(x)={\sum\limits_{\left|\alpha\right|=m}}
   a_{\alpha}x^{\alpha},</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   where
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi>x</mi><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><mi
   mathvariant="normal">…</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><
   mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>∈</mo><msup><mi>𝕂</mi><mi>n</mi></msu
   p></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n}<
   /annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   ,
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi>α</mi><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>α</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><mi
   mathvariant="normal">…</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>α</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><
   mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>∈</mo><msup><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mi>ℕ</mi><mo>∪</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">{</mo><mn>0</mn><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})\in({
   \mathbb{N}}\cup\{0\})^{n}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   ,
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>α</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><mrow><msub><mi>α</mi><mn>1</mn
   ></msub><mo>+</mo><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">⋯</mo><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>α</mi><mi>n</mi></msub></mrow></
   mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">|\alpha|=\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}</ann
   otation></semantics> :MATH]
   ,
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mi>x</mi><msup><mrow></mrow><mi>α</mi></msup><mo>=</m
   o><mi>x</mi><msub><mrow></mrow><mn>1</mn></msub><msup><mrow></mrow><msu
   b><mi>α</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></msup><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">⋯</mo><mi>x</mi><msub><mrow></mrow><mi>n</mi></msub><msup>
   <mrow></mrow><msub><mi>α</mi><mi>n</mi></msub></msup></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">x^{\alpha}=x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots
   x_{n}^{\alpha_{n}}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   and
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><msub><mi>a</mi><mi>α</mi></msub><mo>∈</mo><mi>𝕂</mi><
   /mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">a_{\alpha}\in{\mathbb{K}}</annotation></se
   mantics> :MATH]
   . Here,
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi
   class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒫</mi><mrow><msup><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>m</mi></msup><msup><mi>𝕂</mi><mi>n</mi></msu
   p><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\mathcal{P}}(^{m}{\mathbb{K}}^{n})</annot
   ation></semantics> :MATH]
   stands for the finite dimension linear space of all the homogeneous
   polynomials of degree
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>m</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">m</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   on
   [MATH: <semantics><msup><mi>𝕂</mi><mi>n</mi></msup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\mathbb{K}}^{n}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   .

   If
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mo rspace="0em">∥</mo><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">⋅</mo><mo lspace="0em">∥</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\|\cdot\|</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   is a norm on
   [MATH: <semantics><msup><mi>𝕂</mi><mi>n</mi></msup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\mathbb{K}}^{n}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   , then the formula
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo><mi>P</mi><mo stretchy="false">‖</mo></mrow><mo
   rspace="0.1389em">:=</mo><mrow><mo lspace="0.1389em"
   movablelimits="false" rspace="0em">sup</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo><mrow><mi>P</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo rspace="0.278em"
   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mo
   rspace="0.278em">:</mo><mrow><mi>x</mi><mo>∈</mo><msub><mi>𝖡</mi><mi>X<
   /mi></msub></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><mo>,</mo></mrow><annotatio
   n
   encoding="application/x-tex">\|P\|:=\sup\{|P(x)|:x\in{\mathsf{B}}_{X}\}
   ,</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   for all
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi>P</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi
   class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒫</mi><mrow><msup><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>m</mi></msup><msup><mi>𝕂</mi><mi>n</mi></msu
   p><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">P\in{\mathcal{P}}(^{m}{\mathbb{K}}^{n})</a
   nnotation></semantics> :MATH]
   , where
   [MATH: <semantics><msub><mi>𝖡</mi><mi>X</mi></msub><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\mathsf{B}}_{X}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   is the unit ball of the Banach space
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi>X</mi><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>𝕂</mi><mi>n</mi></msup><mo>,</mo><mo
   lspace="0em" rspace="0em">∥</mo><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">⋅</mo><mo
   lspace="0em" rspace="0.167em">∥</mo><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">X=({\mathbb{K}}^{n},\|\cdot\|)</annotation
   ></semantics> :MATH]
   , defines a norm in
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi
   class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒫</mi><mrow><msup><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>m</mi></msup><msup><mi>𝕂</mi><mi>n</mi></msu
   p><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\mathcal{P}}(^{m}{\mathbb{K}}^{n})</annot
   ation></semantics> :MATH]
   usually called polynomial norm. The space
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi
   class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒫</mi><mrow><msup><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>m</mi></msup><msup><mi>𝕂</mi><mi>n</mi></msu
   p><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\mathcal{P}}(^{m}{\mathbb{K}}^{n})</annot
   ation></semantics> :MATH]
   endowed with the polynomial norm induced by
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>X</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">X</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   is denoted by
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi
   class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒫</mi><mrow><msup><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>m</mi></msup><mi>X</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\mathcal{P}}(^{m}X)</annotation></semanti
   cs> :MATH]
   .

   Other norms customarily used in
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi
   class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒫</mi><mrow><msup><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>m</mi></msup><msup><mi>𝕂</mi><mi>n</mi></msu
   p><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\mathcal{P}}(^{m}{\mathbb{K}}^{n})</annot
   ation></semantics> :MATH]
   besides the polynomial norm are the
   [MATH: <semantics><msub><mi
   mathvariant="normal">ℓ</mi><mi>p</mi></msub><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\ell_{p}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   norms of the coefficients. Namely, if
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>P</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">P</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   is as in (1.1) and
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>≥</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">p\geq 1</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   , then
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>P</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mi>p</mi></msub><mo>:=</mo><msup><mrow><
   mo>(</mo><mrow><munder><mo lspace="0em" movablelimits="false"
   rspace="0em">∑</mo><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>α</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><mi>m</mi></mrow></munder><msup
   ><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mi>α</mi></msub><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mi>p</mi></msup></mrow><mo>)</mo></mrow>
   <mfrac><mn>1</mn><mi>p</mi></mfrac></msup></mrow><mo>,</mo></mrow><anno
   tation
   encoding="application/x-tex">|P|_{p}:=\left(\sum_{|\alpha|=m}|a_{\alpha
   }|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}},</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   defines another norm in
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi
   class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒫</mi><mrow><msup><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>m</mi></msup><msup><mi>𝕂</mi><mi>n</mi></msu
   p><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\mathcal{P}}(^{m}{\mathbb{K}}^{n})</annot
   ation></semantics> :MATH]
   . It is important to point out that although the polynomial norm is,
   most of the times, very difficult to compute, the
   [MATH: <semantics><msub><mi
   mathvariant="normal">ℓ</mi><mi>p</mi></msub><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\ell_{p}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   norm of the coefficients is fairly easy to obtain. Since
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi
   class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒫</mi><mrow><msup><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>m</mi></msup><msup><mi>𝕂</mi><mi>n</mi></msu
   p><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\mathcal{P}}(^{m}{\mathbb{K}}^{n})</annot
   ation></semantics> :MATH]
   is finite dimensional, the polynomial norm
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mo rspace="0em">∥</mo><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">⋅</mo><mo lspace="0em">∥</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\|\cdot\|</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   and the
   [MATH: <semantics><msub><mi
   mathvariant="normal">ℓ</mi><mi>p</mi></msub><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\ell_{p}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   norm
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mo fence="false" stretchy="false">|</mo><mo
   lspace="0em" rspace="0em">⋅</mo><mo fence="false"
   stretchy="false">|</mo><msub><mrow></mrow><mi>p</mi></msub></mrow><anno
   tation
   encoding="application/x-tex">|\cdot|_{p}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   (
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>≥</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">p\geq 1</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   ) are equivalent, and therefore there exist constants
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>m</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>n</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo
   rspace="0.667em">,</mo><mrow><mrow><mi>K</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>m</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>n</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>></mo><mn>0</mn></mrow></mrow>
   <annotation encoding="application/x-tex">k(m,n),\
   K(m,n)>0</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   such that
   (1.2)
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><mrow><mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>m</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>n</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msub><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>P</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mi>p</mi></msub></mrow><mo>≤</mo><mrow><
   mo stretchy="false">‖</mo><mi>P</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo></mrow><mo>≤</mo><mrow><mi>K</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow
   ><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>m</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>n</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><mo
   fence="false">|</mo><msub><mrow><mi>P</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mi>p</mi></msub></mrow><mo>,</mo></mrow>
   <annotation encoding="application/x-tex">k(m,n)|P|_{p}\leq\|P\|\leq
   K(m,n)|P|_{p},</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   for all
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi>P</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi
   class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒫</mi><mrow><msup><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>m</mi></msup><msup><mi>𝕂</mi><mi>n</mi></msu
   p><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">P\in{\mathcal{P}}(^{m}{\mathbb{K}}^{n})</a
   nnotation></semantics> :MATH]
   . The latter inequalities may provide a good estimate on
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">‖</mo><mi>P</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\|P\|</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   as long as we know the exact value of the best possible constants
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>m</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>n</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">k(m,n)</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   and
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi>K</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>m</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>n</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">K(m,n)</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   appearing in (1.2).

   The problem presented above is an extension of the the well known
   polynomial Bohnenblust-Hille inequality (polynomial BH inequality for
   short). It was proved in [5] that there exists a constant
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><msub><mi>D</mi><mi>m</mi></msub><mo>></mo><mn>0</mn><
   /mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">D_{m}>0</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   such that for every
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi>P</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi
   class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒫</mi><mrow><msup><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>m</mi></msup><msubsup><mi
   mathvariant="normal">ℓ</mi><mi
   mathvariant="normal">∞</mi><mi>n</mi></msubsup><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>𝕂</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">P\in{\mathcal{P}}(^{m}\ell_{\infty}^{n}({\
   mathbb{K}}))</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   we have
   (1.3)
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>P</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mfrac><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>m</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>m</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn
   ></mrow></mfrac></msub><mo>≤</mo><mrow><msub><mi>D</mi><mi>m</mi></msub
   ><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo><mi>P</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><mo>,</mo></mrow><annotatio
   n encoding="application/x-tex">|P|_{\frac{2m}{m+1}}\leq
   D_{m}\|P\|,</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   where
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><msubsup><mi mathvariant="normal">ℓ</mi><mi
   mathvariant="normal">∞</mi><mi>n</mi></msubsup><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>ℝ</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\ell_{\infty}^{n}({\mathbb{R}})</annotatio
   n></semantics> :MATH]
   and
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><msubsup><mi mathvariant="normal">ℓ</mi><mi
   mathvariant="normal">∞</mi><mi>n</mi></msubsup><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>ℂ</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\ell_{\infty}^{n}({\mathbb{C}})</annotatio
   n></semantics> :MATH]
   are, respectively, the real and complex versions of
   [MATH: <semantics><msubsup><mi mathvariant="normal">ℓ</mi><mi
   mathvariant="normal">∞</mi><mi>n</mi></msubsup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\ell_{\infty}^{n}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   . Observe that (1.3) coincides with the first inequality in (1.2) for
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo
   lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>m</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>m</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn
   ></mrow></mfrac></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">p=\frac{2m}{m+1}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   except for the fact that
   [MATH: <semantics><msub><mi>D</mi><mi>m</mi></msub><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">D_{m}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   in (1.3) can be chosen in such a way that it is independent from the
   dimension
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>n</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">n</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   . Actually Bohnenblust and Hille showed that
   [MATH: <semantics><mfrac><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>m</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>m</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn
   ></mrow></mfrac><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\frac{2m}{m+1}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   is optimal in (1.3) in the sense that for
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo><</mo><mfrac><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo
   lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>m</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>m</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn
   ></mrow></mfrac></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">p<\frac{2m}{m+1}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   , any constant
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>D</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">D</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   fitting in the inequality
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>P</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mi>p</mi></msub><mo>≤</mo><mrow><mi>D</m
   i><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo><mi>P</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><mo>,</mo></mrow><annotatio
   n encoding="application/x-tex">|P|_{p}\leq
   D\|P\|,</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   for all
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi>P</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi
   class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒫</mi><mrow><msup><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>m</mi></msup><msubsup><mi
   mathvariant="normal">ℓ</mi><mi
   mathvariant="normal">∞</mi><mi>n</mi></msubsup><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>𝕂</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">P\in{\mathcal{P}}(^{m}\ell_{\infty}^{n}({\
   mathbb{K}}))</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   depends necessarily on
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>n</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">n</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   .

   The best constants in (1.3) depend considerably on whether we consider
   the real or the complex version of
   [MATH: <semantics><msubsup><mi mathvariant="normal">ℓ</mi><mi
   mathvariant="normal">∞</mi><mi>n</mi></msubsup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\ell_{\infty}^{n}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   , which motivates the following definition:

Definition 1.1.

   The polynomial Bohnenblus-Hille constant for polynomials of degree
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>m</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">m</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   is defined as
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow><mi>𝕂</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>m<
   /mi></mrow></msub><mo rspace="0.1389em">:=</mo><mrow><mo
   lspace="0.1389em" movablelimits="false"
   rspace="0em">inf</mo><mrow><mo>{</mo><mrow><mi>D</mi><mo>></mo><mn>0</m
   n></mrow><mo lspace="0.278em"
   rspace="0.278em">:</mo><mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>P</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mfrac><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>m</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>m</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn
   ></mrow></mfrac></msub><mo>≤</mo><mrow><mi>D</mi><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">‖</mo><mi>P</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><mtext
   class="ltx_mathvariant_italic">, for
   all </mtext><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi>ℕ</mi></mrow><mtext
   class="ltx_mathvariant_italic"> and </mtext><mrow><mi>P</mi><mo>∈</mo><
   mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒫</mi><mrow><msup><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>m</mi></msup><msubsup><mi
   mathvariant="normal">ℓ</mi><mi
   mathvariant="normal">∞</mi><mi>n</mi></msubsup><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>𝕂</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><mo>}</mo></mrow></mrow></m
   row><mo lspace="0em">.</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">D_{{\mathbb{K}},m}:=\inf\left\{D>0:\text{$
   |P|_{\frac{2m}{m+1}}\leq D\|P\|$, for all $n\in{\mathbb{N}}$ and
   $P\in{\mathcal{P}}(^{m}\ell_{\infty}^{n}({\mathbb{K}}))$}\right\}.</ann
   otation></semantics> :MATH]

   If we restrict attention to a certain subset
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>E</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">E</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   of
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi
   class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒫</mi><mrow><msup><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>m</mi></msup><msubsup><mi
   mathvariant="normal">ℓ</mi><mi
   mathvariant="normal">∞</mi><mi>n</mi></msubsup><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>𝕂</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\mathcal{P}}(^{m}\ell_{\infty}^{n}({\math
   bb{K}}))</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   for some
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi>ℕ</mi></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">n\in{\mathbb{N}}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   , then we define
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow><mi>𝕂</mi><mo>,</mo>
   <mi>m</mi></mrow></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>E</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo
   rspace="0.1389em">:=</mo><mrow><mo lspace="0.1389em"
   movablelimits="false"
   rspace="0em">inf</mo><mrow><mo>{</mo><mrow><mi>D</mi><mo>></mo><mn>0</m
   n></mrow><mo lspace="0.278em"
   rspace="0.278em">:</mo><mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>P</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mfrac><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>m</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>m</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn
   ></mrow></mfrac></msub><mo>≤</mo><mrow><mi>D</mi><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">‖</mo><mi>P</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><mtext
   class="ltx_mathvariant_italic"> for
   all </mtext><mrow><mi>P</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi>E</mi></mrow></mrow><mo>}</mo
   ></mrow></mrow></mrow><mo lspace="0em">.</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">D_{{\mathbb{K}},m}(E):=\inf\left\{D>0:\tex
   t{$|P|_{\frac{2m}{m+1}}\leq D\|P\|$ for all $P\in
   E$}\right\}.</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   For simplicity we will often use the notation
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow><mi>𝕂</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>m</mi></
   mrow></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">D_{{\mathbb{K}},m}(n)</annotation></semant
   ics> :MATH]
   instead of
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow><mi>𝕂</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>m</mi></
   mrow></msub><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi
   class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒫</mi><mrow><msup><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>m</mi></msup><msubsup><mi
   mathvariant="normal">ℓ</mi><mi
   mathvariant="normal">∞</mi><mi>n</mi></msubsup><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>𝕂</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">D_{{\mathbb{K}},m}({\mathcal{P}}(^{m}\ell_
   {\infty}^{n}({\mathbb{K}})))</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   . Note that
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>≤</mo><mrow><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow>
   <mi>𝕂</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>m</mi></mrow></msub><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>≤</mo><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow><m
   i>𝕂</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>m</mi></mrow></msub></mrow><mo>,</mo></mrow><anno
   tation encoding="application/x-tex">1\leq D_{{\mathbb{K}},m}(n)\leq
   D_{{\mathbb{K}},m},</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   for all
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>m</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>n</mi></mrow><mo>∈</mo><
   mi>ℕ</mi></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">m,n\in{\mathbb{N}}</annotation></semantics
   > :MATH]
   .

   A good idea of the asymptotic growth of the constants
   [MATH:
   <semantics><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow><mi>𝕂</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>m</mi></mrow><
   /msub><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">D_{{\mathbb{K}},m}</annotation></semantics
   > :MATH]
   and
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow><mi>𝕂</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>m</mi></
   mrow></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">D_{{\mathbb{K}},m}(n)</annotation></semant
   ics> :MATH]
   is provided by the following definition:

Definition 1.2.

   The asymptotic hypercontractivity constant of the polynomial BH
   inequality is
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>H</mi><mrow><mi>𝕂</mi><mo>,</mo><mi
   mathvariant="normal">∞</mi></mrow></msub><mo
   rspace="0.1389em">:=</mo><mrow><munder><mo lspace="0.1389em"
   movablelimits="false" rspace="0.167em">lim
   sup</mo><mi>m</mi></munder><mroot><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow><mi>𝕂</mi><mo>,
   </mo><mi>m</mi></mrow></msub><mi>m</mi></mroot></mrow></mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">.</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">H_{\mathbb{K},\infty}:=\limsup_{m}\sqrt[m]
   {D_{{\mathbb{K}},m}}.</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   Similarly, if we restrict attention to polynomials in
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>n</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">n</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   variables then we define
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>H</mi><mrow><mi>𝕂</mi><mo>,</mo>
   <mi mathvariant="normal">∞</mi></mrow></msub><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo
   rspace="0.1389em">:=</mo><mrow><munder><mo lspace="0.1389em"
   movablelimits="false" rspace="0.167em">lim
   sup</mo><mi>m</mi></munder><mroot><mrow><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow><mi>𝕂</mi
   ><mo>,</mo><mi>m</mi></mrow></msub><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mi>m</mi></mroot></mrow></mrow><m
   o lspace="0em">.</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">H_{\mathbb{K},\infty}(n):=\limsup_{m}\sqrt
   [m]{D_{{\mathbb{K}},m}(n)}.</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   Of course
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>≤</mo><mrow><msub><mi>H</mi><mrow><mi>𝕂<
   /mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∞</mi></mrow></msub><mo
   lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>≤</mo><msub><mi>H</mi><mrow><m
   i>𝕂</mi><mo>,</mo><mi
   mathvariant="normal">∞</mi></mrow></msub></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">1\leq H_{\mathbb{K},\infty}(n)\leq
   H_{\mathbb{K},\infty}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   , for all
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi>ℕ</mi></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">n\in{\mathbb{N}}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   .

   It was shown in [9] that the complex polynomial Bohnenblust–Hille
   inequality is, at most, hypercontractive. In [2] the estimate on
   [MATH:
   <semantics><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow><mi>ℂ</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>m</mi></mrow><
   /msub><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">D_{{\mathbb{C}},m}</annotation></semantics
   > :MATH]
   was improved. In fact the authors show that for every
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mi>B</mi><mo>></mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">B>1</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   there exists
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mi>A</mi><mo>></mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">A>0</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   such that
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow><mi>ℂ</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>m</mi></
   mrow></msub><mo>≤</mo><mrow><mi>A</mi><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mi>e</mi><mrow><mi>B</mi><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msqrt><mrow><mi>m</mi><mo lspace="0.167em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mi>log</mi><mo
   lspace="0.167em">⁡</mo><mi>m</mi></mrow></mrow></msqrt></mrow></msup></
   mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">D_{{\mathbb{C}},m}\leq Ae^{B\sqrt{m\log
   m}}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   , from which it follows that
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>H</mi><mrow><mi>ℂ</mi><mo>,</mo><mi
   mathvariant="normal">∞</mi></mrow></msub><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>H</mi><mrow><m
   i>ℂ</mi><mo>,</mo><mi
   mathvariant="normal">∞</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><an
   notation
   encoding="application/x-tex">H_{\mathbb{C},\infty}(n)=H_{\mathbb{C},\in
   fty}=1</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   , for all
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi>ℕ</mi></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">n\in{\mathbb{N}}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   . For the real case, it has been recently proved in [7] that
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><msub><mi>H</mi><mrow><mi>ℝ</mi><mo>,</mo><mi
   mathvariant="normal">∞</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow><an
   notation
   encoding="application/x-tex">H_{\mathbb{R},\infty}=2</annotation></sema
   ntics> :MATH]
   . However, not many exact values of
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow><mi>𝕂</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>m</mi></
   mrow></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">D_{{\mathbb{K}},m}(n)</annotation></semant
   ics> :MATH]
   are known so far. This paper is devoted to calculate, explicitly or
   numerically some values of these constants.

   This paper is arranged in two main sections. In Section 2, we employ
   some results on the geometry of spaces of polynomials in order to
   provide the exact value of
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow><mi>ℂ</mi><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn></
   mrow></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>2</mn><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">D_{\mathbb{C},2}(2)</annotation></semantic
   s> :MATH]
   . In Section 3 we use a similar technique to find the exact value of
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow><mi>ℝ</mi><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn></
   mrow></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>2</mn><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">D_{\mathbb{R},2}(2)</annotation></semantic
   s> :MATH]
   . We also provide lower estimates for
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow><mi>ℝ</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>m</mi></
   mrow></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>2</mn><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">D_{{\mathbb{R}},m}(2)</annotation></semant
   ics> :MATH]
   and
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><msub><mi>H</mi><mrow><mi>ℝ</mi><mo>,</mo><mi
   mathvariant="normal">∞</mi></mrow></msub><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>2</mn><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">H_{{\mathbb{R}},\infty}(2)</annotation></s
   emantics> :MATH]
   by means of numerical calculus.

   The polynomial Bohnenblust–Hille inequality has important applications
   in different fields of Mathematics and Physics and has been studied in
   depth by many authors since a multilinear version of the
   Bohnenblust–Hille inequality was proved in 1931 (see [2, 3, 6, 12, 11,
   13, 14, 5, 4, 15, 16, 10, 19, 21, 25, 23, 17, 20, 22, 24]) and the
   references therein.

2. The exact value of
[MATH:
<semantics><mrow><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow><mi>ℂ</mi><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow><
/msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo
stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>2</mn><mo
stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
encoding="application/x-tex">D_{\mathbb{C},2}(2)</annotation></semantics>
:MATH]

   Throughout this section we will often identify any two-variable
   polynomial
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>a</mi><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mi>z</mi><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow><mo>+</mo><m
   row><mi>b</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>w</mi><mo
   lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>z</mi></mrow><mo>+</mo><mrow><mi>c</mi><mo
   lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mi>w</mi><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow></mrow><anno
   tation
   encoding="application/x-tex">az^{2}+bwz+cw^{2}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   or any one-variable polynomial
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>a</mi><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mi>λ</mi><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow><mo>+</mo><m
   row><mi>b</mi><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>λ</mi></mrow><mo>+</mo><mi>c</mi></mrow><annotat
   ion
   encoding="application/x-tex">a\lambda^{2}+b\lambda+c</annotation></sema
   ntics> :MATH]
   , for
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mi>a</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>b</mi><mo>,</mo><mrow><mi>c</m
   i><mo>∈</mo><mi>𝕂</mi></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">a,b,c\in{\mathbb{K}}</annotation></semanti
   cs> :MATH]
   , with the vector
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>a</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>b</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>c</m
   i><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>∈</mo><msup><mi>𝕂</mi><mn>3</mn></msu
   p></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">(a,b,c)\in{\mathbb{K}}^{3}</annotation></s
   emantics> :MATH]
   . Also, we use the standard notation
   [MATH: <semantics><msub><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo><mrow><mrow><mi>a</mi><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mi>z</mi><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow><mo>+</mo><m
   row><mi>b</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>w</mi><mo
   lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>z</mi></mrow><mo>+</mo><mrow><mi>c</mi><mo
   lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mi>w</mi><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo></mrow><mi>𝔻</mi></msub><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\|az^{2}+bwz+cw^{2}\|_{\mathbb{D}}</annota
   tion></semantics> :MATH]
   for the supremum of
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo><mrow><mrow><mi>a</mi><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mi>z</mi><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow><mo>+</mo><m

   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">|az^{2}+bwz+cw^{2}|</annotation></semantic
   s> :MATH]
   for
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mi>z</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>w</mi></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">z,w</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   in the unit disk
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>𝔻</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\mathbb{D}}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   of
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>ℂ</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\mathbb{C}}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   . Similarly,
   [MATH: <semantics><msub><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo><mrow><mrow><mi>a</mi><mo lspace="0em"

   encoding="application/x-tex">\|a\lambda^{2}+b\lambda+c\|_{\mathbb{D}}</

   stands for the supremum of
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo><mrow><mrow><mi>a</mi><mo lspace="0em"

   rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>λ</mi></mrow><mo>+</mo><mi>c</mi></mrow><mo

   encoding="application/x-tex">|a\lambda^{2}+b\lambda+c|</annotation></se
   mantics> :MATH]
   for
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mi>λ</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi>𝔻</mi></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\lambda\in{\mathbb{D}}</annotation></seman
   tics> :MATH]
   . Observe that
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo><mrow><mrow><mi>a</mi><mo lspace="0em"

   n
   encoding="application/x-tex">\|az^{2}+bwz+cw^{2}\|_{\mathbb{D}}=\|a\lam
   bda^{2}+b\lambda+c\|_{\mathbb{D}}=\max_{|\lambda|=1}|a\lambda^{2}+b\lam
   bda+c|,</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   being the last of the latter equalities due to the Maximum Modulus
   Principle.

   The main result of this section depends upon the following lemma, which
   is of independent interest.

Lemma 2.1.

   Let
   [MATH:

Proof.

   If we perform the change of variables
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mrow><mi>z</mi><mo

   in
   [MATH: <semantics><msub><mrow><mo

   encoding="application/x-tex">|a^{\prime}\lambda^{2}+b^{\prime}\lambda+c

    1. (1)
       Assume first

       [MATH: <semantics><mrow><msub><mrow><mo

   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mrow><mrow><msqrt><mn>2</mn></msqrt><mo

    2. (2)
       Assume next
       [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi>a</mi><mo>≥</mo><mrow><mo

       [MATH: <semantics><mrow><msup><mi>a</mi><mo>′</mo></msup><mo

       In this case, choose

   [MATH:

   [MATH:

       Hence, we will achieve the desired result if we can guarantee

   /mstyle><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo

   encoding="application/x-tex">\displaystyle+2\left[ac\left(1-2\left({a-c

   2}|b|\sqrt{1-\left({a-c\over|b|}\right)^{2}}\right],</annotation></sema
   ntics> :MATH]
       and (in this case) we will be working with the condition

   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>≤</mo><mrow><msubsup><mi

Theorem 2.3.

   The optimal complex polynomial Bohnenblust-Hille constant for
   polynomials in
   [MATH: <semantics><msub><mi>E</mi><mi>ℝ</mi></msub><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">E_{\mathbb{R}}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   , which we denote by
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow><mi>ℂ</mi><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn></
   mrow></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>E</mi><mi>ℝ</mi></msub><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">D_{{\mathbb{C}},2}(E_{\mathbb{R}})</annota
   tion></semantics> :MATH]
   , is given by
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow><mi>ℂ</mi><mo>,</mo><mn>2<
   /mn></mrow></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>E</mi><mi>ℝ</mi></msub><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>=</mo><mroot><mfrac><mn>3</mn>
   <mn>2</mn></mfrac><mn>4</mn></mroot></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">D_{{\mathbb{C}},2}(E_{\mathbb{R}})=\sqrt[4
   ]{\frac{3}{2}}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   . Moreover,
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow><mi>ℂ</mi><mo>,</mo>
   <mn>2</mn></mrow></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>2</mn><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>=</mo><mroot><mfrac><mn>3</mn>
   <mn>2</mn></mfrac><mn>4</mn></mroot><mo>≈</mo><mn>1.1066</mn></mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">.</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">D_{{\mathbb{C}},2}(2)=\sqrt[4]{\frac{3}{2}
   }\approx 1.1066.</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

Proof.

   Using convexity we have
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow><mi>ℂ</mi><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn></
   mrow></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>E</mi><mi>ℝ</mi></msub><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\displaystyle
   D_{{\mathbb{C}},2}(E_{\mathbb{R}})</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mphantom></mphantom><mo
   rspace="0.1389em">=</mo><mrow><mo lspace="0.1389em"
   movablelimits="false" rspace="0em">sup</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">{</mo><msub><mrow><mo stretchy="false">‖</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>a</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>b</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>c</m
   i><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo rspace="0.278em"
   stretchy="false">‖</mo></mrow><mfrac><mn>4</mn><mn>3</mn></mfrac></msub
   ><mo rspace="0.278em">:</mo><mrow><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo><mrow><mrow><mi>a</mi><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msubsup><mi>z</mi><mn>1</mn><mn>2</mn></msubsup></m
   row><mo>+</mo><mrow><mi>b</mi><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msub><mi>z</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msub><mi>z</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></mrow><mo>+</mo><m
   row><mi>c</mi><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msubsup><mi>z</mi><mn>2</mn><mn>2</mn></msubsup></m
   row></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo></mrow><mo>≤</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\displaystyle=\sup\{\|(a,b,c)\|_{\frac{4}{
   3}}:\|az_{1}^{2}+bz_{1}z_{2}+cz_{2}^{2}\|\leq
   1\}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mphantom></mphantom><mo
   rspace="0.1389em">=</mo><mrow><mo lspace="0.1389em"
   movablelimits="false" rspace="0em">sup</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">{</mo><msub><mrow><mo stretchy="false">‖</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>a</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>b</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>c</m
   i><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo rspace="0.278em"
   stretchy="false">‖</mo></mrow><mfrac><mn>4</mn><mn>3</mn></mfrac></msub
   ><mo rspace="0.278em">:</mo><mrow><msub><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>a</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>b</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>c</m
   i><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo></mrow><mi>ℂ</mi></msub><mo>≤</mo><mn>1</mn></mr
   ow><mo stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\displaystyle=\sup\{\|(a,b,c)\|_{\frac{4}{
   3}}:\|(a,b,c)\|_{\mathbb{C}}\leq 1\}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mphantom></mphantom><mo

Remark 3.1.

   Throughout this section we will compute several times norms of
   polynomials on the real line numerically. This is done by using Matlab.
   In particular, if
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi>P</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">P(x)</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   is a real polynomial on
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>ℝ</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\mathbb{R}}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   , we apply the predefined Matlab function roots.m to
   [MATH: <semantics><msup><mi>P</mi><mo>′</mo></msup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">P^{\prime}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   in order to obtain an approximation of all the critical points of
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>P</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">P</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   . If
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><mi
   mathvariant="normal">…</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><
   /mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}</annotation></semantics
   > :MATH]
   are all the roots of
   [MATH: <semantics><msup><mi>P</mi><mo>′</mo></msup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">P^{\prime}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   in
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mrow><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">[-1,1]</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   , then we approach the norm of
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>P</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">P</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   as
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mo rspace="0.167em">∥</mo><mi>P</mi><mo
   lspace="0em" rspace="0.0835em">∥</mo><mo
   lspace="0.0835em">:=</mo><mi>max</mi><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">{</mo><mo fence="false" rspace="0.167em"

Theorem 3.3.

   Let
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>f</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">f</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   be the real valued function given by
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mrow><mi>f</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo

   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>=</mo><msup><mrow><mo>[</mo><m

   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></msqrt></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mfrac><mn>4</mn><mn>3</mn></mfrac></msup
   ></mrow><mo>]</mo></mrow><mfrac><mn>3</mn><mn>4</mn></mfrac></msup></mr
   ow><mo lspace="0em">.</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">f(t)=\left[2t^{\frac{4}{3}}+(2\sqrt{t(1-t)
   })^{\frac{4}{3}}\right]^{\frac{3}{4}}.</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   We have that
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow><mi>ℝ</mi><mo>,</mo><mn>2<
   /mn></mrow></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>2</mn><mo

Proof.

   Let
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mrow><mi>f</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>=</mo><msup><mrow><mo>[</mo><m
   row><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mi>t</mi><mfrac><mn>4</mn><mn>3</mn></mfrac><
   /msup></mrow><mo>+</mo><msup><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msqrt><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>−</mo><mi>t</mi></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></msqrt></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mfrac><mn>4</mn><mn>3</mn></mfrac></msup
   ></mrow><mo>]</mo></mrow><mfrac><mn>3</mn><mn>4</mn></mfrac></msup></mr
   ow><mo lspace="0em">.</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">f(t)=\left[2t^{\frac{4}{3}}+(2\sqrt{t(1-t)
   })^{\frac{4}{3}}\right]^{\frac{3}{4}}.</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   We just have to notice that due to the convexity of the
   [MATH: <semantics><msub><mi
   mathvariant="normal">ℓ</mi><mi>p</mi></msub><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\ell_{p}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   -norms and Theorem 3.2 we have
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow><mi>ℝ</mi><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn></
   mrow></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>2</mn><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\displaystyle
   D_{{\mathbb{R}},2}(2)</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mo rspace="0.1389em">=</mo><mo
   lspace="0.1389em" movablelimits="false" rspace="0em">sup</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">{</mo><mo fence="false" rspace="0.167em"
   stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>𝐚</mi><msub><mo fence="false"

   stretchy="false">|</mo><mfrac><mn>4</mn><mn>3</mn></mfrac></msub><mo
   rspace="0.278em">:</mo><mi>𝐚</mi><mo>∈</mo><msub><mi>𝖡</mi><mrow><mi

   mi>ℝ</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msub><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\displaystyle=\sup\{|\mathbf{a}|_{\frac{4}
   {3}}:\mathbf{a}\in{\mathsf{B}}_{{\mathcal{P}}(^{2}\ell_{\infty}^{2}{\ma
   thbb{R}})}\}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mo rspace="0.1389em">=</mo><mo
   lspace="0.1389em" movablelimits="false" rspace="0em">sup</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">{</mo><mo fence="false" rspace="0.167em"
   stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>𝐚</mi><msub><mo fence="false"
   rspace="0.167em"

   rspace="0.278em">:</mo><mi>𝐚</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi>ext</mi><mrow><mo

   lspace="0em">.</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\displaystyle=\sup\{|\mathbf{a}|_{\frac{4}
   {3}}:\mathbf{a}\in\ext({\mathsf{B}}_{{\mathcal{P}}(^{2}\ell_{\infty}^{2
   }{\mathbb{R}})})\}=\sup_{t\in[1/2,1]}f(t).</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]

   Some calculations will show that the last supremum is attained at
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>t</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><
   /mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">t=t_{0}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   , concluding the proof. ∎

   Now, if
   [MATH: <semantics><msub><mi>𝐚</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\mathbf{a}_{n}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   is the vector of the coefficients of
   [MATH:
   <semantics><msubsup><mi>P</mi><mn>2</mn><mi>n</mi></msubsup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">P_{2}^{n}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   for each
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi>ℕ</mi></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">n\in{\mathbb{N}}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   , then we know that
   (3.1)
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow><mi>ℝ</mi><mo>,</mo>
   <mrow><mn>2</mn><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>n</mi></mrow></mrow></msub><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>2</mn><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>≥</mo><mfrac><msub><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo><msub><mi>𝐚</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mfrac><mrow><mn>4</mn><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>n</mi></mrow><mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo
   lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>n</mi></mrow><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></mfrac>
   </msub><msup><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo><msub><mi>P</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup></mfrac></mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">.</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">D_{{\mathbb{R}},2n}(2)\geq\frac{|\mathbf{a
   }_{n}|_{\frac{4n}{2n+1}}}{\|P_{2}\|^{n}}.</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]

   Since
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo><msub><mi>P</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\|P_{2}\|=1</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   , then (3.1) with
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>300</mn></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">n=300</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   (see also Figure 4) proves that
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow><mi>ℝ</mi><mo>,</mo>
   <mn>600</mn></mrow></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>2</mn><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>≥</mo><msup><mrow><mo

   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mn>600</mn></msup></mrow><mo>,</mo></mro
   w><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">D_{\mathbb{R},600}(2)\geq(1.36117)^{600},<
   /annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   providing numerical evidence showing that
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>H</mi><mrow><mi>ℝ</mi><mo>,</mo>
   <mi mathvariant="normal">∞</mi></mrow></msub><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>2</mn><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>≥</mo><mn>1.36117</mn></mrow><
   mo lspace="0em">.</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">H_{{\mathbb{R}},\infty}(2)\geq
   1.36117.</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

3.2. Educated guess for the exact calculation of
[MATH:
<semantics><mrow><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow><mi>ℝ</mi><mo>,</mo><mn>3</mn></mrow><
/msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo
stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>2</mn><mo
stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
encoding="application/x-tex">D_{{\mathbb{R}},3}(2)</annotation></semantics>
:MATH]

   To the authors’ knowledge the calculation of
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">‖</mo><mi>P</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\|P\|</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   is, in general, far from being easy. However there is a way to compute
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">‖</mo><mi>P</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\|P\|</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   for specific cases. For instance Grecu, Muñoz and Seoane prove in [18,
   Lemma 3.12] the following formula:

Lemma 3.4.

   If for every
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>a</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>b</mi></mrow><mo>∈</mo><
   mi>ℝ</mi></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">a,b\in{\mathbb{R}}</annotation></semantics
   > :MATH]
   we define
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>P</mi><mrow><mi>a</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>b<
   /mi></mrow></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>y</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>=</mo><mrow><mrow><mi>a</mi><m
   o lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mi>x</mi><mn>3</mn></msup></mrow><mo>+</mo><m
   row><mi>b</mi><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mi>x</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>y</mi></mrow><mo>+</mo><mrow><mi>b</mi><mo
   lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>x</mi><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mi>y</mi><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow><mo>+</mo><m
   row><mi>a</mi><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mi>y</mi><mn>3</mn></msup></mrow></mrow></mro
   w><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">P_{a,b}(x,y)=ax^{3}+bx^{2}y+bxy^{2}+ay^{3}
   </annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   then
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo><msub><mi>P</mi><mrow><mi>a</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>b<
   /mi></mrow></msub><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo>{</mo><mtable
   columnspacing="5pt" displaystyle="true" rowspacing="0pt"><mtr><mtd
   class="ltx_align_left"
   columnalign="left"><mrow><mo>|</mo><mrow><mrow><mi>a</mi><mo>−</mo><mst
   yle
   displaystyle="false"><mfrac><msup><mi>b</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mrow><mn>
   3</mn><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>a</mi></mrow></mfrac></mstyle></mrow><mo>+</mo><
   mstyle displaystyle="false"><mfrac><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mi>b</mi><mn>3</mn></msup></mrow><mrow><mn>27
   </mn><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mi>a</mi><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow></mfrac></ms
   tyle><mo>+</mo><mrow><mstyle
   displaystyle="false"><mfrac><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>a</mi></mrow><mn>27</mn></mfrac></mstyle><mo
   lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mrow><mo>(</mo><mrow><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">−</mo><mstyle
   displaystyle="false"><mfrac><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>b</mi></mrow><mi>a</mi></mfrac></mstyle></mrow><

   <mi>a</mi><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mi>x</mi><mn>5</mn></msup></mrow><mo>−</mo><m

   encoding="application/x-tex">P_{5}(x,y)=ax^{5}-bx^{4}y-cx^{3}y^{2}+cx^{
   2}y^{3}+bxy^{4}-ay^{5},</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   with
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>a</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\displaystyle a</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   [MATH:

   tics> :MATH]

   The norm of
   [MATH: <semantics><msub><mi>P</mi><mn>5</mn></msub><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">P_{5}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   can be calculated numerically (using Remark 3.1), and it turns out to
   be
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mrow><mo

   up to
   [MATH: <semantics><mn>5</mn><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">5</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   decimal places. The authors have numerical evidence showing that
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow><mi>ℝ</mi><mo>,</mo>

   mo lspace="0em">.</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">D_{{\mathbb{R}},5}(2)\approx
   6.83591.</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   In any case we have
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow><mi>ℝ</mi><mo>,</mo>
   <mn>5</mn></mrow></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo

   6.83591.</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   It is interesting to observe that we can improve numerically the
   estimate
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>H</mi><mrow><mi
   mathvariant="normal">∞</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>ℝ</mi></mrow></msub><mo

   encoding="application/x-tex">H_{\infty,{\mathbb{R}}}(2)\geq\sqrt[8]{27}
   \approx 1.50980</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   (see [7, Theorem 4.2]) by considering polynomials of the form
   [MATH:
   <semantics><msubsup><mi>P</mi><mn>5</mn><mi>n</mi></msubsup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">P_{5}^{n}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   [MATH: <semantics><msub><mi>𝐚</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><annotation

   :MATH]
   is the vector of the coefficients of
   [MATH:

   encoding="application/x-tex">P_{5}^{n}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   for each
   [MATH:

   encoding="application/x-tex">n\in{\mathbb{N}}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   , then we know that
   (3.3)
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow><mi>ℝ</mi><mo>,</mo>
   <mrow><mn>5</mn><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>n</mi></mrow></mrow></msub><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>2</mn><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>≥</mo><mfrac><msub><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo><msub><mi>𝐚</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mfrac><mrow><mn>10</mn><mo lspace="0em"

   </msub><msup><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo><msub><mi>P</mi><mn>5</mn></msub><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup></mfrac></mrow><mo>,</mo
   ></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">D_{{\mathbb{R}},5n}(2)\geq\frac{|\mathbf{a
   }_{n}|_{\frac{10n}{5n+1}}}{\|P_{5}\|^{n}},</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]

   Using (3.3) with
   [MATH:

   [MATH:

   w><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">D_{\mathbb{R},600}(2)\geq(1.54987)^{600},<
   /annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   providing numerical evidence showing that
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>H</mi><mrow><mi>ℝ</mi><mo>,</mo>

3.4. Educated guess for the exact calculation of
[MATH:
<semantics><mrow><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow><mi>ℝ</mi><mo>,</mo><mn>6</mn></mrow><
/msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo
stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>2</mn><mo
stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
encoding="application/x-tex">D_{{\mathbb{R}},6}(2)</annotation></semantics>
:MATH]

   The authors have numerical evidence pointing to the fact that an
   extreme polynomial in the Bohnenblust-Hille inequality for polynomials
   in
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi
   class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒫</mi><mrow><msup><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>6</mn></msup><msubsup><mi
   mathvariant="normal">ℓ</mi><mi
   mathvariant="normal">∞</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>ℝ</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\mathcal{P}}(^{6}\ell_{\infty}^{2}({\math
   bb{R}}))</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   may be of the form
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>Q</mi><mrow><mi>a</mi><mo>,</mo>
   <mi>b</mi></mrow></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>y</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>=</mo><mrow><mrow><mi>a</mi><m
   o lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mi>x</mi><mn>5</mn></msup><mo
   lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>y</mi></mrow><mo>+</mo><mrow><mi>b</mi><mo
   lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mi>x</mi><mn>3</mn></msup><mo
   lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mi>y</mi><mn>3</mn></msup></mrow><mo>+</mo><m
   row><mi>a</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>x</mi><mo
   lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mi>y</mi><mn>5</mn></msup></mrow></mrow></mro
   w><mo lspace="0em">.</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">Q_{a,b}(x,y)=ax^{5}y+bx^{3}y^{3}+axy^{5}.<
   /annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   This motivates a deeper study of this type of polynomials, which we do
   in the following result.

Theorem 3.6.

   Let
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>Q</mi><mrow><mi>a</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>b<

   rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mi>y</mi><mn>5</mn></msup></mrow></mrow></mro
   w><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">Q_{a,b}(x,y)=ax^{5}y+bx^{3}y^{3}+axy^{5}</
   annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   for
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>a</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>b</mi></mrow><mo>∈</mo><
   mi>ℝ</mi></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">a,b\in{\mathbb{R}}</annotation></semantics
   > :MATH]
   and consider the subspace of
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi
   class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒫</mi><mrow><msup><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>6</mn></msup><msubsup><mi
   mathvariant="normal">ℓ</mi><mi
   mathvariant="normal">∞</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>ℝ</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\mathcal{P}}(^{6}\ell_{\infty}^{2}({\math
   bb{R}}))</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   given by
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi>F</mi><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">{</mo><msub><mi>Q</mi><mrow><mi>a</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>b<
   /mi></mrow></msub><mo lspace="0.278em"
   rspace="0.278em">:</mo><mi>a</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>b</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi>ℝ</m
   i><mo stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">F=\{Q_{a,b}:a,b\in{\mathbb{R}}\}</annotati
   on></semantics> :MATH]
   . Suppose
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><msub><mi>λ</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo><</mo><msub><mi>λ
   </mi><mn>1</mn></msub></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\lambda_{0}<\lambda_{1}</annotation></sema
   ntics> :MATH]
   are the only two roots of the equation
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mrow><mfrac><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo><mrow><mrow><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mi>λ</mi><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow><mo>−</mo><m
   n>20</mn></mrow><mo>+</mo><mrow><mi>λ</mi><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msqrt><mrow><mrow><mn>9</mn><mo lspace="0em"

   n>20</mn></mrow></msqrt></mrow></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mn>25</mn></mfrac><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msqrt><mfrac><mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mrow><mn>3</mn>
   <mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>λ</mi></mrow></mrow><mo>−</mo><msqrt><mrow><mrow
   ><mn>9</mn><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mi>λ</mi><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow><mo>−</mo><m
   n>20</mn></mrow></msqrt></mrow><mn>10</mn></mfrac></msqrt></mrow><mo>=<
   /mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>λ</mi></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow></mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">.</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\frac{|3\lambda^{2}-20+\lambda\sqrt{9\lamb
   da^{2}-20}|}{25}\sqrt{\frac{-3\lambda-\sqrt{9\lambda^{2}-20}}{10}}=|2+\
   lambda|.</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   Then if
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mi>λ</mi><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mi>b</mi><mi>a</mi></mfrac
   ></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\lambda=\frac{b}{a}</annotation></semantic
   s> :MATH]
   we have
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mfrac><msub><mrow><mo

   n>0</mn></mrow><mtext
   class="ltx_mathvariant_italic"> and </mtext><mrow><msub><mi>λ</mi><mn>0

   d></mtr><mtr><mtd><mrow><mstyle

   </mo><msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>λ</mi><mo

   mtable><mphantom></mphantom></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\frac{|(0,a,0,b,0,a,0)|_{\frac{12}{7}}}{\|
   Q_{a,b}\|}=\left\{\begin{array}[c]{c}\frac{25\sqrt{10}\left(2+|\lambda|
   ^{\frac{12}{7}}\right)^{\frac{12}{7}}}{\left|3\lambda^{2}-20+\lambda\sq
   rt{9\lambda^{2}-20}\right|\sqrt{-3\lambda-\sqrt{9\lambda^{2}-20}}},\tex
   t{ if $a\neq 0$ and $\lambda_{0}<\frac{b}{a}<\lambda_{1}$},\\
   \frac{\left(2+|\lambda|^{\frac{12}{7}}\right)^{\frac{7}{12}}}{|2+\lambd

   :MATH]

   Observe that
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><msub><mi>λ</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo
   rspace="0em">≈</mo><mrow><mo

   encoding="application/x-tex">\lambda_{0}\approx-2.2654</annotation></se
   mantics> :MATH]
   ,
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><msub><mi>λ</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo
   rspace="0em">≈</mo><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">−</mo><mn>1.6779</mn></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\lambda_{1}\approx-1.6779</annotation></se
   mantics> :MATH]
   and the above function attains its maximum when
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mfrac><mi>b</mi><mi>a</mi></mfrac><mo>=</mo><msub><mi
   >λ</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\frac{b}{a}=\lambda_{0}</annotation></sema
   ntics> :MATH]
   (see Figure 3), which implies that
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow><mi>ℝ</mi><mo>,</mo>
   <mn>6</mn></mrow></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>F</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>=</mo><mfrac><msup><mrow><mo>(
   </mo><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo><msup><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo><msub><mi>λ</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mfrac><mn>12</mn><mn>7</mn></mfrac></msu
   p></mrow><mo>)</mo></mrow><mfrac><mn>12</mn><mn>7</mn></mfrac></msup><m
   row><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>λ</mi><mn>0<
   /mn></msub></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow></mfrac><mo>≈</mo><mn>10.7809</mn></mrow>
   <mo lspace="0em">.</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">D_{{\mathbb{R}},6}(F)=\frac{\left(2+|\lamb
   da_{0}|^{\frac{12}{7}}\right)^{\frac{12}{7}}}{|2+\lambda_{0}|}\thickapp
   rox 10.7809.</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

Proof.

   We do not lose generality by considering only polynomials of the form
   [MATH:
   <semantics><msub><mi>Q</mi><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>λ</mi></mrow><
   /msub><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">Q_{1,\lambda}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   , in which case
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo><msub><mi>Q</mi><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>λ<
   /mi></mrow></msub><mo stretchy="false">‖</mo></mrow><mo
   rspace="0.1389em">=</mo><mrow><mo lspace="0.1389em"
   movablelimits="false" rspace="0em">sup</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo><mrow><msup><mi>x</mi><mn>5</mn></msup><mo>+</mo
   ><mrow><mi>λ</mi><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mi>x</mi><mn>3</mn></msup></mrow><mo>+</mo><m
   i>x</mi></mrow><mo rspace="0.278em" stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mo
   rspace="0.278em">:</mo><mrow><mi>x</mi><mo>∈</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">[</mo><mrow><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">.</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\|Q_{1,\lambda}\|=\sup\{|x^{5}+\lambda
   x^{3}+x|:x\in[0,1]\}.</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   The polynomial
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>q</mi><mi>λ</mi></msub><mo
   lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>:=</mo><mrow><msup><mi>x</mi><
   mn>5</mn></msup><mo>+</mo><mrow><mi>λ</mi><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mi>x</mi><mn>3</mn></msup></mrow><mo>+</mo><m
   i>x</mi></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">q_{\lambda}(x):=x^{5}+\lambda
   x^{3}+x</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   has no critical points if
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi>λ</mi><mo rspace="0em">></mo><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">−</mo><mfrac><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msqrt><mn>5</mn></msqrt></mrow><mn>3</mn></mfrac></
   mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\lambda>-\frac{2\sqrt{5}}{3}</annotation><
   /semantics> :MATH]
   , otherwise it has the following critical points in
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">[</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn><mo
   stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">[0,1]</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   :
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>:=</mo><msq
   rt><mfrac><mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>λ</mi></mrow></mrow><mo>−</mo><msqrt><mrow><mrow
   ><mn>9</mn><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mi>λ</mi><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow><mo>−</mo><m
   n>20</mn></mrow></msqrt></mrow><mn>10</mn></mfrac></msqrt></mrow><mspac
   e style="width:1em;" width="1em"></mspace><mtext>and</mtext><mspace
   style="width:1em;"
   width="1em"></mspace><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>:=</mo>
   <msqrt><mfrac><mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>λ</mi></mrow></mrow><mo>+</mo><msqrt><mrow><mrow
   ><mn>9</mn><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mi>λ</mi><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow><mo>−</mo><m

   e style="width:1em;"

   lspace="0em">−</mo><mfrac><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msqrt><mn>5</mn></msqrt></mrow><mn>3</mn></mfrac></
   mrow></mrow><mtext>,</mtext></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">x_{0}:=\sqrt{\frac{-3\lambda-\sqrt{9\lambd
   a^{2}-20}}{10}}\quad\text{and}\quad
   x_{1}:=\sqrt{\frac{-3\lambda+\sqrt{9\lambda^{2}-20}}{10}}\quad\text{if
   $-2\leq\lambda\leq-\frac{2\sqrt{5}}{3}$,}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]

   and
   [MATH: <semantics><msub><mi>x</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">x_{0}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   if
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi>λ</mi><mo rspace="0em">≤</mo><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">−</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\lambda\leq-2</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   . Notice that
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><msub><mi>q</mi><mi>λ</mi></msub><mo
   lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\displaystyle
   q_{\lambda}(x_{0})</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mphantom></mphantom><mo>=</mo><mrow><mstyle
   displaystyle="true"><mfrac><mrow><mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mrow><mn>3</mn>
   <mo lspace="0em"

   tation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\displaystyle=\frac{-3\lambda^{2}+20-\lamb
   da\sqrt{9\lambda^{2}-20}}{20}x_{0}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><msub><mi>q</mi><mi>λ</mi></msub><mo
   lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo

   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\displaystyle
   q_{\lambda}(x_{1})</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><mphantom></mphantom><mo>=</mo><mrow><mstyle
   displaystyle="true"><mfrac><mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo
   lspace="0em"

   </mo><mn>20</mn><mo>+</mo><mrow><mi>λ</mi><mo lspace="0em"

   lspace="0em"

   encoding="application/x-tex">\displaystyle=\frac{-3\lambda^{2}+20+\lamb

   It is easy to check that
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo><mrow><msub><mi>q</mi><mi>λ</mi></msub><mo
   lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo

   stretchy="false">|</mo><mrow><msub><mi>q</mi><mi>λ</mi></msub><mo
   lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo

   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">|q_{\lambda}(x_{0})|\geq|q_{\lambda}(x_{1}
   )|</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   for
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow><mo>≤</mo><mi>λ</mi><
   mo rspace="0em">≤</mo><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">−</mo><mfrac><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msqrt><mn>5</mn></msqrt></mrow><mn>3</mn></mfrac></
   mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">-2\leq\lambda\leq-\frac{2\sqrt{5}}{3}</ann
   otation></semantics> :MATH]
   , which implies that
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo><msub><mi>Q</mi><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>λ<
   /mi></mrow></msub><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo>{</mo><mtable
   columnspacing="5pt" displaystyle="true" rowspacing="0pt"><mtr><mtd
   class="ltx_align_left"

   stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>λ</mi></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mo>,</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo><mrow><msub><mi>q</mi><mi>λ</mi></msub><mo
   lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo

   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></mrow></mtd><mtd class="ltx_align_left"
   columnalign="left"><mrow><mtext>if </mtext><mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>2<
   /mn></mrow><mo>≤</mo><mi>λ</mi><mo rspace="0em">≤</mo><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">−</mo><mfrac><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo lspace="0em"

   mrow></mrow><mtext>,</mtext></mrow></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd
   class="ltx_align_left" columnalign="left"><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>λ</mi></mrow><mo

   columnalign="left"><mtext>otherwise.</mtext></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow
   ></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\|Q_{1,\lambda}\|=\begin{cases}\max\{|2+\l
   ambda|,|q_{\lambda}(x_{0})|\}&\text{if
   $-2\leq\lambda\leq-\frac{2\sqrt{5}}{3}$,}\\
   |2+\lambda|&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]

   The equation
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>λ</mi></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo

   on></semantics> :MATH]
   turns out to have only two roots, namely
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><msub><mi>λ</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo

   and
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><msub><mi>λ</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo

   mantics> :MATH]
   . By continuity, it is easy to prove that
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>λ</mi></mrow><mo

   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">|2+\lambda|\leq|q_{\lambda}(x_{0})|</annot
   ation></semantics> :MATH]
   only if
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow><mo>≤</mo><mi>λ</mi><
   mo rspace="0em">≤</mo><mrow><mo

   mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">-2\leq\lambda\leq-\frac{2\sqrt{5}}{3}</ann
   otation></semantics> :MATH]
   , which concludes the proof. ∎

   As mentioned above, we have numerical evidence showing that
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow><mi>ℝ</mi><mo>,</mo>

   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>=</mo><mrow><msub><mi>D</mi><m
